70.24348pt\raise 4.49997pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 70.24348pt\raise-15.99991pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hbox{\cirbuild@}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-10.99568pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\Psi_{j}}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 20.9957pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.99571pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-6.04515pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{}^{{\rm B}}_{\phantom{{\rm B}}}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 40.99573pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.99574pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.00002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{c_{k}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}=p(j,i,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})

(8)

associated with possible outcomes
j,i,k
, and where we explicitly
show the dependence of the joint probability distribution
p(j,i,k)

on the tests composing the circuit.

Within this scenario, Bob can use both the test
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
and
any observation test
\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
in order to extract the
information on the inputs, while he can use both the test

\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
and any preparation test
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
in order
to extract the information on the outputs. This leaves room for two
inequivalent conditions for a no-information test
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
.

1.

Strong condition for no-information test
.

(a)

No-information on inputs
: the test
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
is
no-information on inputs if for every preparation test

\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
and for every observation tests
\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
,
the joint probability in Eq. (
8
) factorizes as

\displaystyle p(j,i,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})

\displaystyle=r(i|k;\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})s(j,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z}),

namely we impose that

r(i|j,k;\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})=r(i|k;\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})
,
where the probability distribution
r
does not depend on the
preparation test
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
(we remind that it may happen that
a probability distribution depends on a given test but not on its
outcomes). The interpretation of this condition is that the outcomes
of
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
and their correlations with the outcomes of any
observation test do not provide information on the preparation.

(b)

No-information on outputs
: the test

\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
is no-information on outputs if for every

\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
and for every
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
the joint probability
in Eq. (
8
) factorizes as

\displaystyle p(j,i,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})

\displaystyle=r(i|j;\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X})s(j,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z}),

where the probability distribution
r
does not depend on the
observation test
\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
. This condition ensures that the outcomes of

\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
and their correlations with the outcomes of any
preparation test do not provide information on the observation.

2.

Weak condition for no-information test
.

(a)

No-information on inputs
: the test
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
is
no-information on inputs if for every preparation test

\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
and for every observation test
\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
, the
joint probability in Eq. (
8
) is such that

\displaystyle\sum_{k}p(j,i,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})

(9)

\displaystyle=r(i|\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})s(j|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z}),

where the probability distribution
r
does not depend on the
preparation test
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
. The interpretation of this
condition is that the outcomes of
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
do not provide
information on the preparation, whenever we ignore the outcome of the
observation test.

(b)

No-information on outputs
: the test
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
is
no-information on outputs if for every observation test

\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
and for every preparation test
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
, the
joint probability in Eq. (
8
) is such that

\displaystyle\sum_{j}p(j,i,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})=

(10)

\displaystyle r(i|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X})s(k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z}),

where the probability distribution
r
does not depend on the
observation test
\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
. This means that the outcomes of
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}

do not provide information on the observation, whenever we ignore
the outcome of the preparation test.

It is elementary to see
that
1a
\Rightarrow
2a

(and
1b
\Rightarrow
2b
), namely
the strong no-information condition implies the weak one, both on
inputs and outputs. In the literature on no-information without
disturbance in quantum theory the authors take the weak
notion
2a
of no-information test (only on
inputs since the quantum theory is causal). Here, we also choose the
conditions
2
as expressed in the next definition (the
equivalence between the weak conditions
2
and
Definition
15
is proved in Appendix
B
). The motivation for this choice is that if an OPT satisfies
no-information without disturbance according to conditions
2
,
then it also satisfies no-information without disturbance in the
strongest sense of conditions
1
(see
Remark
4
in the following).

Definition 15

(No-information test)
.

A test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
with events
\mathcal{A}_{i}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm C})

is a no-information test if, for every choice of deterministic
effect
e_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}
and deterministic state
\omega_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
, there
exists a deterministic effect
f_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
and a deterministic state

\nu_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}
such that for every
i\in{\rm X}
one has

\displaystyle\left(e\right|_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}=p_{i}(e)\left(f\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=q_{i}(\omega)\left|\nu\right)_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}.

(12)

According to Eq. (
11
) (that coincides with
Eq. (
9
) in the weak condition of
item
2a
), the test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
does
not provide information upon any possible input state. However, the
probability distribution
p_{i}(e)
might in principle provide
information about the effect
e
. On the other hand according to
Eq. (
12
) (that coincides with
Eq. (
10
) in the weak condition of
item
2b
), the test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
does
not provide information upon output of any possible effect, while the
probability distribution
q_{i}(\omega)
might in principle provide
information about the state
\omega
. The conjunction of the two
conditions implies that no-information is provided by the test about

D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
and
D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
, namely about any
possible input state and output effect of any dilated system. The last
statement is proved in the following lemma.

Lemma 1
.

Let the test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
with events

\mathcal{A}_{i}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm C})
be a no-information test. Then one has

\displaystyle\left(e\right|_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}=r_{i}\left(f\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=r_{i}\left|\nu\right)_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}.

(14)

Proof.

By Eqs. (
11
) and (
12
) one has

\displaystyle\left(e\right|_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=p_{i}(e)=q_{i}(\omega)=r_{i},

where we used the fact that
e,f
and
\omega,\nu
are respectively
deterministic effects and deterministic states.

\,\blacksquare

Remark 3
.

Notice that in Eq. (
11
) the probability of the
transformation
\mathcal{A}_{i}

\forall i\in{\rm X}
generally depends on the
deterministic effect
e_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}
, this accounting for non-causal
theories. In the more general case in which also the deterministic
effect
f_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
on the right hand side of
Eq. (
11
) depends on
i\in{\rm X}
, the test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
would provide information on the system state
(this would happen, however, only for probabilistic states). An
analogous argument holds for
\nu
in
Eq. (
12
).

3.1
No-information without disturbance

In this section we state the condition of no-information without
disturbance and introduce criteria for it to be satisfied by an OPT.

Definition 16

(OPT with no-information without disturbance)
.

We say that an OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance if,
for every system
{\rm A}
, and every test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
, if the test is
non-disturbing then it is a no-information test.

Theorem 1
.

An OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance if and only if
the identity transformation is atomic for every system of the
theory.

Proof.

We start proving that if an OPT satisfies no-information without
disturbance then the identity transformation is atomic. Consider a
system
{\rm A}
of the theory, and a refinement
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}

(
\mathcal{A}_{i}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
for every
i\in{\rm X}
) of the identity map

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
for system
{\rm A}
. The test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
is clearly non-disturbing, therefore by
hypothesis it is a no-information test. By definition of
no-information test, and using Lemma
1
, we know that
for every deterministic effect
e_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}
, and deterministic state

\omega_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
, there exists a deterministic effect
f_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}

and a deterministic state
\nu_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
such that for every

i\in{\rm X}
one has
\left(e\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}=r_{i}\left(f\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
, and

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=r_{i}\left|\nu\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
. Summing both sides
of the last equation over the index
i\in{\rm X}
, and remembering that

\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}r_{i}=1
, we find that
e=f
and

\omega=\nu
. Therefore, the no-information condition is

\displaystyle\left(e\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}=r_{i}\left(e\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=r_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(16)

for every deterministic effect
e_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
and for every
deterministic state
\omega_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
. Consider now an arbitrary
pure state
\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm B})}
(the same proof can be done
choosing an arbitrary atomic effect
c\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A}{\rm B})}
). Since

\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\lambda_{i}(\Psi)\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},\quad\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}(\Psi)=1,

(17)

where the coefficients
\lambda_{i}(\Psi)
generally depend on the state

\Psi
. However, for each pure state
\Psi
there exists a
deterministic effect
e^{\Psi}\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A}{\rm B})}
such that

(e^{\Psi}|\Psi)\neq 0
. Upon applying the deterministic effect

e^{\Psi}
on both sides of Eq. (
17
), we get

\displaystyle(e^{\Psi}|\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\lambda_{i}(\Psi)(e^{\Psi}|\Psi)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}.

(18)

Now, applying both sides of Eq. (
15
) to
\Psi
, we get

\displaystyle(e^{\Psi}|\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=r_{i}(e^{\Psi}|\Psi)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(19)

and comparing the last two identities, considering that

(e^{\Psi}|\Psi)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\neq 0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}(\Psi)=r_{i},\qquad\forall i\in{\rm X}.

(20)

Since this holds true for every pure state
\Psi
, we conclude that

\lambda_{i}(\Psi)
is independent of
\Psi
. Then

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\rho\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=r_{i}\left|\rho\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
,

\forall\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm B})
, proving that

\mathcal{A}_{i}=r_{i}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
. Notice that we implicitly assumed that the
probabilities
r_{i}
do not depend on the choice of the system

{\rm B}
. Actually this can be proven as shown in Appendix
C
.

The converse implication, namely that if in an OPT the identity
transformation is atomic then a non-disturbing test is no-information,
is trivial.

\,\blacksquare

Remark 4
.

Eq. (
8
) shows the most general scenario in which a
test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
can be used to extract information on its
inputs or on its outputs. We noticed that two inequivalent
definitions of no-information tests are possible, a strong
condition
1
, and a weak condition
2
, depending
on the features of the joint probability distribution
p(j,i,k)
of
Eq. (
8
). However, due to the above theorem, if a
theory satisfies no-information without disturbance in the weak
sense, then a non-disturbing test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
is such that

\mathcal{A}_{i}=q_{i}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
, with
\sum_{i}q_{i}=1
. It follows that in
Eq. (
8
) the joint probability distribution

p(j,i,k)
is of the form
p(j,i,k)=q_{i}p(j,k)
, and the test is also
no-information in the strong sense.

Besides the atomicity of the identity, we can provide other two
equivalent necessary and sufficient conditions for no-information
without disturbance.

Proposition 3
.

An OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance if and only if
for every system there exists an atomic transformation which is
either left- or right-reversible.

Proof.

We start proving that a theory with an atomic reversible
transformation for each system satisfies no-information without
disturbance. Let
\mathcal{R}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm C})
be atomic and
left-reversible (the right-reversible case is analogous). Then
consider a refinement
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, with

\mathcal{A}_{i}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
for
i\in{\rm X}
, of the identity
transformation. By definition of identity map we have that

\mathcal{R}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{R}
, and due to the atomicity of
\mathcal{R}

it must be
\mathcal{R}\mathcal{A}_{i}\propto\mathcal{R}
for every
i\in{\rm X}
. Since
\mathcal{R}
is
left-reversible (namely there exists
\mathcal{W}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm C},{\rm A})
such
that
\mathcal{W}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
) it follows that
\mathcal{A}_{i}\propto\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
for
every
i\in{\rm X}
, which proves the atomicity of
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
.

The other implication, that in a theory that satisfies no-information
without disturbance for every system there exists an atomic
transformation which is either left- or right-reversible, is
trivial. Indeed, in a theory that satisfies no-information without
disturbance the identity, which is both right- and left-reversible, is
atomic as proved in Theorem
1
.

\,\blacksquare

Proposition 4
.

An OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance if and only if for every
system every reversible transformation is atomic.

Proof.

We prove that if the theory satisfies no-information without
disturbance, then every reversible transformation is atomic.
Indeed, let
\mathcal{R}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
be reversible, and suppose that

\mathcal{R}=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\mathcal{R}_{i}
for test
\{\mathcal{R}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
. Then, one
has

\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\mathcal{R}_{i}\mathcal{R}^{-1}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm A},

(21)

and by Theorem
1
one has that

\mathcal{R}_{i}\mathcal{R}^{-1}=p_{i}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
. Finally, multiplying by
\mathcal{R}
to the
right, we conclude that
\mathcal{R}_{i}=p_{i}\mathcal{R}
, namely the refinements of

\mathcal{R}
must be trivial. For the converse, it is sufficient to observe
that the identity is reversible.

\,\blacksquare

3.2
Information without disturbance

In this section we provide the general structure of the state spaces
and effect spaces of any theory where some information can be extracted
from a system without introducing disturbance. Such information is
“classical” in the sense that the measurement is the reading of
information that is repeatable and shareable. In particular, for the
classical OPT the whole information encoded on a system can be read in
this way. The proof of the above statements are based on the
following theorem.

Theorem 2
.

The non redundant atomic refinement of
the identity is unique for every
system. Moreover, given the non redundant atomic refinement

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
of the identity

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, one has
\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\delta_{ij}
.

Proof.

Suppose that the identity transformation of system
{\rm A}

allows for two atomic refinements
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, and

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{j\in{\rm Y}}\mathcal{B}_{j}
. Since
\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{B}_{j}=\mathcal{B}_{j}
, from
the atomicity of the transformations
\mathcal{B}_{j}
we get

\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{B}_{j}=c_{ij}\mathcal{B}_{j}
, for some
c_{ij}\geq 0
such that

\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}c_{ij}=1

\forall j\in{\rm Y}
. Similarly we get

\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{B}_{j}=d_{ij}\mathcal{A}_{i}
for some
d_{ij}\geq 0
such that

\sum_{j\in{\rm Y}}d_{ij}=1

\forall i\in{\rm X}
. Then

c_{ij}\mathcal{B}_{j}=d_{ij}\mathcal{A}_{i}
. By non redundancy one has that for fixed

j
there is only one value of
i=i(j)
such that
c_{ij}>0
, and
normalisation gives
c_{i(j)j}=1
. By a similar argument for a fixed

i
there is
j(i)
such that
d_{ij(i)}=1
. Then one has

\mathcal{B}_{j}=\mathcal{A}_{i(j)}
. This proves the uniqueness of the non redundant
atomic refinement of the identity.

By the same argument as before, for the non redundant atomic
refinement of the identity one has

\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{A}_{j}=c_{ij}\mathcal{A}_{j}=d_{ij}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, for some

c_{ij},d_{ij}\geq 0
such that

\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}c_{ij}=\sum_{j\in{\rm X}}d_{ij}=1

\forall i,j\in{\rm X}
.
By atomicity and non redundancy one must have

c_{ij}=d_{ij}=\delta_{i,j}
.

\,\blacksquare

The above theorem has as a consequence the following structure theorem
for OPTs.

Corollary 1
.

For any pair of systems
{\rm A}
,
{\rm B}
of an OPT one has the following
decomposition of the set of states and of the set of effects of

{\rm A}{\rm B}

\displaystyle{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm B})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}{\mathsf{St}}_{ij}({\rm A}{\rm B}),

(22)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A}{\rm B})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{(i,j)\in{\rm X}\times{\rm Y}}{\mathsf{Eff}}_{ij}({\rm A}{\rm B}),

where for non redundant atomic decompositions
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
,

\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}
of the identities
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
and
\mathcal{I}_{\rm B}
, one
has

\displaystyle(\mathcal{A}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{j})\left|\Psi_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\left|\Psi_{ij}\right),

(23)

\displaystyle\left(c_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\right|(\mathcal{A}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{j})

\displaystyle=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\left(c_{ij}\right|,

for all
\Psi_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}({\rm A}{\rm B})
and

c_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}\in{\mathsf{Eff}}_{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}}({\rm A}{\rm B})
.

Remark 5
.

Notice that from Eq. (
22
) it trivially follows that for
any system
{\rm A}
the block decomposition holds

{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})=\bigoplus_{i\in{\rm X}}{\mathsf{St}}_{i}({\rm A}),\quad{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A})=\bigoplus_{i\in{\rm X}}{\mathsf{Eff}}_{i}({\rm A}).

(24)

However, Eq. (
22
) contains the additional information that
the decomposition holds in that specific form also for composite
systems. This is not a straightforward consequence of the
decomposition of local states and local effects, as witnessed by the
Fermionic case. Indeed, the state in Eq. (
4
) does not have
definite parity for the two subsystems corresponding to two Fermions
on the left and one on the right, hence the state space cannot be of
the form in Eq. (
22
).

Remark 6
.

For a theory without atomicity of parallel composition it is
possibile that the refinement
\mathcal{A}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{j}
in
Eq. (
23
) of
\mathcal{I}_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
is not atomic. In such a case
one has
{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm B})=\bigoplus_{k\in{\rm Z}}{\mathsf{St}}_{k}({\rm A}{\rm B})
, and

{\mathsf{St}}_{ij}({\rm A}{\rm B})=\bigoplus_{k\in{\rm Z}_{ij}}{\mathsf{St}}_{k}({\rm A}{\rm B})
, for
some partition
{\rm Z}_{ij}
of
{\rm Z}
.

3.2.1
Full information without disturbance

In the following we formalise the fact that a theory where any
information can be extracted via a non-disturbing test must have only
classical systems. Let us first define the notion of full-information
without disturbance.

Definition 17

(Full-information without
disturbance)
.

An OPT satisfies full-information without disturbance if for every
system
{\rm A}
and every test

\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
there exists a non
disturbing test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
(namely

\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\mathcal{I}_{\rm A})
such that

\mathcal{B}_{j}=\sum_{i}p(j|i)\,\mathcal{V}_{ij}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{R}_{ij},

(25)

for some probability distribution
p
and reversible transformations

\mathcal{V}_{ij},\mathcal{R}_{ij}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
.

As a consequence of the above definition we have the following lemma
on the structure of atomic maps in a theory with full-information
without disturbance.

Lemma 2
.

Consider an OPT with full-information without disturbance. Any
atomic transformation
\mathcal{B}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
is of the form

\mathcal{B}=\lambda\,\mathcal{U}\mathcal{A}_{k}=\lambda\,\mathcal{A}_{l}\mathcal{U},

where
\mathcal{A}_{k},\mathcal{A}_{l}
are atomic transformations in the unique
non-redundant refinement of the identity
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
of
Theorem
2
,
\mathcal{U}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
is a reversible
transformation and
\lambda\geq 0
.

Proof.

Consider a test
\{\mathcal{B}_{j}\}_{j\in{\rm Y}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
such
that
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{j}
for some
j\in{\rm Y}
. Due to full-information without
disturbance (see Eq. (
25
) in Definition
17

where we now take as non disturbing test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
the
unique non-redundant atomic refinement of the identity

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
) and to the atomicity of
\mathcal{B}
one has

\mathcal{B}=\lambda\,\mathcal{V}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{R}
, for some
i\in{\rm X}
,
\lambda\geq 0
and

\mathcal{V},\mathcal{R}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
reversible transformations. Consider
now the three tests
\{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{R}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
,

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{V}\mathcal{R}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
and
\{\mathcal{V}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
, and
observe that

\sum_{i}\mathcal{V}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{R}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{V}\mathcal{R}\mathcal{A}_{i}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathcal{V}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{V}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{U}
,
with
\mathcal{U}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
reversible. We then conclude the proof
noticing that by Theorem
2
the non redundant atomic
refinement of a reversible transformation is unique.

\,\blacksquare

We can now state the main Theorem of this section.

Theorem 3
.

If an OPT is full-information without disturbance then every system
of the theory is classical.

Proof.

Consider an arbitrary system
{\rm A}
of the theory. Since by
hypothesis the identity is not atomic, let
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
be
the unique non-redundant atomic refinement of the identity

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
of Theorem
2
. Due to Corollary

1
(and the immediately following remark) the sets of
states and effects decompose as in Eq. (
24
). We now
prove that all the blocks in such decompositions must be
one-dimensional. To this end we show that any pair of states

\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i}({\rm A})
is such that

\rho^{\prime}\propto\rho
.

First we show that if an OPT satisfies full-information without
disturbance then for every non null atomic
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i}{({\rm A})}

and atomic
a\in{\mathsf{Eff}}_{i}{({\rm A})}
, one has
(a|\rho)\neq 0
.
Given such
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i}{({\rm A})}
and
a\in{\mathsf{Eff}}_{i}{({\rm A})}

consider the transformation
\left|\rho\right)\left(a\right|\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
, that
is generally not atomic. Due to Lemma
2
all the
atomic refinements of the above transformation are of the form

\left|\rho\right)\left(a\right|=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\,\mathcal{U}_{j}\mathcal{A}_{i},

(26)

where each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
is the element of the non redundant refinement of
the identity
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\rho\right)=\left|\rho\right)
, and

\left(a\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}=\left(a\right|
,
\lambda_{j}>0
, and the
\mathcal{U}_{j}
are reversible
trasformations. Applying both sides of Eq. (
26
) to the
state
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i}{({\rm A})}
one has

\left|\rho\right)(a|\rho)=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\,\mathcal{U}_{j}\left|\rho\right)
. Reminding
that the
\mathcal{U}_{j}
are all reversible,
\lambda_{j}>0
and
\rho
is
non-null, one concludes that the right hand side cannot be null, and
this proves that also the pairing
(a|\rho)
is non-null.

Let us apply the transformation in Eq. (
26
) to another
arbitrary atomic state
\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i}{({\rm A})}
. Since

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\rho^{\prime}\right)=\left|\rho^{\prime}\right)
one finds

\left|\rho\right)(a|\rho^{\prime})=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\,\mathcal{U}_{j}\left|\rho^{\prime}\right)
,
for some
\lambda_{j}>0
and
\mathcal{U}_{j}
reversible transformations. As
shown above it is
(a|\rho^{\prime})\neq 0
, and using the atomicity
of
\rho
one has
\left|\rho^{\prime}\right)\propto{\mathcal{U}_{j}^{-1}}\left|\rho\right)
for
every
j
. Since this holds true for every atomic state

\rho^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{St}}_{i}{({\rm A})}
, one has proved that all atomic states
(and then all states) in
{\mathsf{St}}_{i}{({\rm A})}
are proportional to the
same atomic state, let’s say
{\mathcal{U}_{j_{0}}^{-1}}\left|\rho\right)
for some

j_{0}
. Via an analogous argument one can see that all effects in

{\mathsf{Eff}}_{i}{({\rm A})}
are proportional to the same atomic effect.

\,\blacksquare

Remark 7
.

We remind that a system is classical when all its pure states are
jointly perfectly discriminable. In this case the base of the conic
hull of the pure states of each system is a simplex, which
corresponds to a subset of the set of states for a convex theory. A
special case of theory whose systems are all classical is the usual
classical information theory, where indeed one has full-information
without disturbance. On the other hand, even when all systems are
classical, the theory can differ from classical information theory
e. g. in the rule for systems composition. For example there exist
OPTs whose systems are all clssical but that do not satisfy local
discriminability (see Ref.
[
25
]
).

4
Information and disturbance with restricted input and
output

In this section we extend our previous results to study the relation
between disturbance and information when both input states and output
effects are limited to some given subsets. To this end we first
introduce the basics notion of identical transformations upon
restricted input and output resources.

4.1
Operational identities between transformations

As expressed in Eq. (
1
), two transformations

\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
of an OPT are said to be
operationally equal if for every system
{\rm C}
and for every state

\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
one has

\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}
. However, two
non-identical maps
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
could behave in
the same way when their action is restricted to a relevant subclass of
states.

The notion of identical transformation upon input of a state

\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
has been already introduced in the literature
(see
Refs.
[
26
,
9
]
and
references therein):

Definition 18

(Equal transformations upon input of

\rho
)
.

We say that two transformations
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})

are equal upon input of
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
, and write

\mathcal{A}=_{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, if for every
\sigma\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}
we have that

\mathcal{A}\sigma=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\sigma
.

Remark 8

(Operational interpretation of equality upon input)
.

The equality upon input of a state
\rho
was originally introduced
for quantum theory in Ref.
[
26
]
, where the
authors extended the equality to the whole support of the chosen
density matrix
\rho
. Within the OPT framework the equality upon
input of
\rho
is instead extended to the refinement set

{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}

[
9
]
. This choice
can be easily motivated in operational terms: the equality

\mathcal{A}=_{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
means that the two maps
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

are indistinguishable on the state
\rho
, independently of how it
has been prepared. Suppose that the state
\rho
is prepared by
Alice as
\rho=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\sigma_{i}
, for some refinement of

\rho
. Even Alice, using her knowledge of the preparation cannot
distinguish between
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

From Proposition
1
we know that the local
action of a map is sufficient to determine the map itself if the OPT
satisfies local discriminability (see Definition

9
). However, for theories without local
discriminability the local action of a transformation might not be
sufficient to characterize it. According to
Definition
18
, then, even if

\mathcal{A}=_{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, still the maps
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
could act
differently upon input of dilations of
\rho
, namely it could be

\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}\neq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}
, for some

\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
. In this case the difference between
\mathcal{A}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
would go undetected if their action on system
{\rm A}

only is considered. For this reason we introduce the notion of equal
transformations upon input of dilations of a state
\rho
.

Definition 19

(Equal transformations upon
input of
D_{\rho}
)
.

Given a state
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
, we say that two transformations

\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
are equal upon input of

D_{\rho}
, and write
\mathcal{A}=_{D_{\rho}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, if

\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}
for every

\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
.

Notice that the above definition requires that two transformations act
in the same way on the set
{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
. Due to the absence of
no-restriction of preparation tests, it is not true in general that

D_{{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}}={\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
, as one might expect. The only
inclusion that can be proved without further assumptions is

{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}}
, (see Lemma
5

in Appendix
D
).

Here we show that the two
Definitions
18
and

19
coincide for causal OPTs
with local discriminability. For this purpose we first need the
following lemma.

Lemma 3
.

In a causal OPT, if
\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm B})}
, with

\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
for some
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}
, then

\{\left(b\right|_{{\rm B}}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}|b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}\}\subseteq{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}.

(27)

Proof.

Since
\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
there exists a preparation test

\{\Psi,\bar{\Psi},\Lambda\}\subseteq{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm B})
such that

\Psi+\bar{\Psi}\in D_{\rho}
. For an arbitrary
b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm B})
,
thanks to causality that ensures the existence of a unique
deterministic effect (see Definition
13
), one can
construct the test

\displaystyle\{\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},\left(e-b\right|_{\rm B}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

\displaystyle\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\left|\bar{\Psi}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},\left(e-b\right|_{\rm B}\left|\bar{\Psi}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

\displaystyle\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\left|\Lambda\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},\left(e-b\right|_{\rm B}\left|\Lambda\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\},

where
e_{\rm B}
is the unique deterministic effect. Since the
coarse-graining of the first four elements is
\rho
, we conclude that

\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}
.

\,\blacksquare

Proposition 5
.

In a causal OPT with local discriminability, given two
transformations
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
, the two
conditions
\mathcal{A}=_{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}=_{D_{\rho}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
are
equivalent.

Proof.

We first prove that

\mathcal{A}=_{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathcal{A}=_{D_{\rho}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Consider
an arbitrary
\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
, with

\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}
. Let for example be

\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
. By hypothesis we have that

\mathcal{A}=_{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, namely

\mathcal{A}\left|\sigma\right)_{\rm A}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left|\sigma\right)_{\rm A}
for every

\sigma\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}
. Then, due to Lemma
3
,

\forall b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}
,
\forall c\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm C})}
, we have
that

\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\left(c\right|_{\rm C}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\rm C})\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}=\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\left(c\right|_{\rm C}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\rm C})\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}
,
and by local discriminability (see Definition

9
) we conclude that

(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\rm C})\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}=(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{\rm C})\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}
.
Since this holds true for every
\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
, we conclude
that
\mathcal{A}=_{D_{\rho}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. The converse implication

\mathcal{A}=_{D_{\rho}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\Rightarrow\mathcal{A}=_{\rho}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is
trivial.

\,\blacksquare

Dealing also with non-causal theories, where the role of states and
effects is interchangeable (see Remark
1
) it is
in order to introduce the counterpart of the above definition with
effects replacing states. Accordingly we define equal transformations
upon output of dilations of an effect
a
.

Definition 20

(Equal transformations upon output of

D_{b}
)
.

Given an effect
b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm B})
, we say that two transformations

\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
are equal upon output of
D_{b}
,
and write
\mathcal{A}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.99051pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.72124pt{D_{b}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.99051pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.72124pt{D_{b}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-21.38718pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-11.19545pt{D_{b}}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-20.2983pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-10.10657pt{D_{b}}\kern 4.99846pt}}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, if

\left(c\right|_{{\rm B}{\rm C}}\mathcal{A}=\left(c\right|_{{\rm B}{\rm C}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
for every

c\in{{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{b}}}
.

In the most general case one can define equality of two
transformations when both states and effects are limited to two given
subsets.

Definition 21

(Equal transformations upon
X,Y
)
.

We say that two transformations
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})

are equal upon input of
X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
and upon
output of
Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}}
—or simply upon

X,Y
—and write
\mathcal{A}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-15.18951pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.00816pt{Y}\kern 6.08734pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-14.10063pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.91928pt{Y}\kern 4.99846pt{X}}}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, if

\begin{aligned} \lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern
