(28)

for every
\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{X}
and for every
c\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{Y}
.

As a special case, given a state
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
, and an effect

b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}
, two transformations

\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
are equal upon
D_{\rho},D_{b}
when
in the last definition we take
X=D_{\rho}
and
Y=D_{b}
. Accordingly,
also Definitions
19
and

20
are special cases of
Definition
21
, with

\mathcal{A}=_{D_{\rho}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
corresponding to the choice

X=D_{\rho},Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}}
and

\mathcal{A}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.99051pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.72124pt{D_{b}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.99051pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.72124pt{D_{b}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-21.38718pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-11.19545pt{D_{b}}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{D_{b}}}}}=^{{\kern-20.2983pt\mathchoice{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.35667pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.19498pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.997pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-10.10657pt{D_{b}}\kern 4.99846pt}}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
corresponding to the choice

X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}},Y=D_{b}
. Naturally, also the notion of equal
transformations
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is the one of
Definition
21
with no restrictions on
the set states and effects, that is
X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
,

Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}}
.

Based on the above identities of transformations, any property of an
OPT can be generalized in the upon
X,Y
scenario. Here we only
present the case of two properties that will be used to derive the
following results.

In Definition
5
we introduced the notion of
atomic events, and we can provide a weaker version of the property of
atomicity for transformations.

Definition 22

(Atomic and refinable transformation
upon
X,Y
.)
.

A transformation
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm B})
is atomic upon input of

X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
and upon output of

Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}}
—or simply upon
X,Y
—if all its
refinements are trivial upon
X,Y
, namely
\mathcal{B}\prec\mathcal{A}
implies

\mathcal{B}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-15.18951pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.00816pt{Y}\kern 6.08734pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-14.10063pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.91928pt{Y}\kern 4.99846pt{X}}}}\lambda\mathcal{A}
,
\lambda\in[0,1]
.
Conversely, we say that an event is refinable upon
X,Y
whenever it
is not atomic upon
X,Y

Again, we mention as a special case the atomicity upon

X=D_{\rho},Y=D_{b}
for some state
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}
and effect

b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}
, which in turn reduces to atomicity upon input of

D_{\rho}
(atomicity upon output of
D_{b}
) when

X=D_{\rho},Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}}
(
X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}},Y=D_{b}
).
Similarly, the usual notion of atomicity corresponds to the choice

X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}},Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}}
.

Finally, the usual notion of faithful state and faithful effect (see
Definition
8
) can be generalized to the notion of
faithful state and faithful effect upon input of
X
and upon output
of
Y
.

Definition 23

(Faithful state and faithful effect upon

X,Y
)
.

Consider two arbitrary transformations

\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A},{\rm B})}
and two subsets

X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
and
Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}}
.
A state
\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
is faithful upon input of
X
and
upon output of
Y
—or simply upon
X,Y
—if the condition

\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}=\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}
implies

\mathcal{A}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-15.18951pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.00816pt{Y}\kern 6.08734pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-14.10063pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.91928pt{Y}\kern 4.99846pt{X}}}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Analogously, an effect

d\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B}{\rm C})}
is faithful upon
X,Y
if the condition

\left(d\right|_{{\rm B}{\rm C}}\mathcal{A}=\left(d\right|_{{\rm B}{\rm C}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
implies

\mathcal{A}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-15.18951pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.00816pt{Y}\kern 6.08734pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-14.10063pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.91928pt{Y}\kern 4.99846pt{X}}}}\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.

The case of faithful state and faithful effect of
Definition
8
corresponds to the choice

X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}},Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}({{\rm B}})}
in the above definition.

4.2
Information and disturbance upon
X,Y

We start generalizing Definition
14
of non-disturbing test:

Definition 24

(Non-disturbing test upon
X,Y
)
.

Consider a test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
on system
{\rm A}
. We say that
the test is non-disturbing upon input of

X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
and upon output of

Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
—or simply upon
X,Y
—if

\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-21.1138pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-12.45987pt{Y}\kern 8.41515pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-15.18951pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-9.00816pt{Y}\kern 6.08734pt{X}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{Y}}}}=^{{\kern-14.10063pt\mathchoice{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.42296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.93361pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80971pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.10211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.44402pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1743pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.91928pt{Y}\kern 4.99846pt{X}}}}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}.

(29)

According to Definition
14
a test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}

is
non-disturbing
if it is operationally equal to the identity
transformation of system
{\rm A}
; this is a special case of the above
definition when
X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
, and
Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
.

We can now determine if a test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm C})
provides information
upon
X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})}
,
Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm C})}
. We
first observe that the prescription upon
X,Y
establishes that (i) we
are only interested in getting information on states in
X
and on
effects in
Y
, and (ii) we can only use preparations that involve
states in
X
and measurements that involve effects in
Y
in order to
extract the information. However, a test containing a state in
X

(or an effect in
Y
) may involve events in
\widehat{X}

(
\widehat{Y}
), where the set
\widehat{Z}
is the coexistent
completion of the set
Z
as in
Definition
6
.

Let us focus on the scheme in Eq. (
8
), with the
state in the set
X
and effect in the set
Y
. A test that directly
provides information on
X
may give different probability
distributions for different elements of
X
, given that one measures
an effect
c\in Y
. However, the test could also provide information
about
X
indirectly. This is the case, for example, when for every
test
\{c_{k}\}_{k\in{\rm Z}}\subseteq\widehat{Y}
, one has

p(j,i|k)=p(i)p(j)
for
\Psi_{i}\in X
, while factorisation does not
occur for
\Psi_{i}\in\widehat{X}\setminus X
. A similar situation can
occur when the information is about
Y
, with the roles of preparation
and measurement exchanged.

We thus generalize Definition
15
of no-information test
as follows:

Definition 25

(No-information test upon

X,Y
)
.

A test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
with events
\mathcal{A}_{i}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm C})

is a no-information test upon input of

X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
and upon output of

Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm C})}}
—or simply upon
X,Y
—if for
every choice of deterministic effect
e_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\in\widehat{Y}
and
deterministic state
\omega_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\in\widehat{X}
, there exists a
deterministic effect
f_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
and a deterministic state

\nu_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}
such that for every
i\in{\rm X}
one has

\displaystyle\left(e\right|_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}=_{\widehat{X}}p_{i}(e)\left(f\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.42075pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-11.75403pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-27.33186pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-10.66515pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 4.99846pt}}}q_{i}(\omega)\left|\nu\right)_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}.

(31)

As a special case the above definition coincides with
Definition
15
, corresponding to
X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
,
and
Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
. Notice that in this case it is also

\widehat{X}=X
and
\widehat{Y}=Y
.

According to Eq. (
30
), the test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
does not provide information upon input of

\widehat{X}
once the observations are limited to
\widehat{Y}

(
e_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\in\widehat{Y}
). However, the probability distribution

p_{i}(e)
might in principle provide information about the effect

e
. On the other hand according to Eq. (
31
), the test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
does not provide information upon output of

\widehat{Y}
, once the preparations are limited to
\widehat{X}

(
\omega_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\in\widehat{X}
), while the probability distribution

q_{i}(\omega)
might in principle provide information about the state

\omega
. The conjunction of the two conditions implies that
no-information is provided by the test about
\widehat{X}
and

\widehat{Y}
:

Lemma 4
.

Let the test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
with events

\mathcal{A}_{i}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A},{\rm C})
be a no-information test upon
X,Y
. Then
one has

\displaystyle\left(e\right|_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}=_{\widehat{X}}r_{i}\left(f\right|_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.42075pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-11.75403pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-27.33186pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-10.66515pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 4.99846pt}}}r_{i}\left|\nu\right)_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}.

(33)

Proof.

By Eqs. (
30
) and (
31
), and
remembering that
\omega_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\in\widehat{X}
,

e_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\in\widehat{Y}
, one has

\displaystyle\left(e\right|_{{\rm C}{\rm B}}\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=p_{i}(e)=q_{i}(\omega)=r_{i},

where we used the fact that
e,f
and
\omega,\nu
are respectively
deterministic effects and deterministic states.

\,\blacksquare

Here we state the condition of no-information without disturbance upon

X,Y
:

Definition 26

(No-information without disturbance upon
X,Y
)
.

Consider a system
{\rm A}
and two subsets

X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
,
Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
.
Then the OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance upon input
of
X
and upon output of
Y
—or simply upon
X,Y
—if for every
test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
that is
non-disturbing upon
X,Y
, the test does not provide information
upon
X,Y
.

Clearly the above definition generalizes Definition
16

which corresponds to the choice
X=D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
, and

Y=D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
, namely the theory satisfies no-information
without disturbance and any informative test necessary disturbs.

All the results presented in Section
3.1
on no-information
without disturbance can now be extended to the “upon
X,Y
”
scenario. The result of Theorem
1
, proving the atomicity
of the identity as a necessary and sufficient condition for
no-information without disturbance, is generalized by the following
theorem:

Theorem 4
.

An OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance upon

\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
, with
X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
,

Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
, if and only if the identity

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
is atomic upon
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
.

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of that of Theorem
1
. In
the present case Eq. (
17
) for pure state in

\widehat{X}
holds upon
\widehat{Y}
. Now, if we apply on both
sides a deterministic effect
e_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}\in\widehat{Y}
, using
Eq. (
32
) it may happen that
(e|\Psi)=0
. The case
where this happens for every
e\in\widehat{Y}
corresponds to a state

\Psi
which is equal to the null state upon

\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
. Considering the remaining cases one
concludes that

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\rho\right)\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.42075pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt}}_{{\kern-11.75403pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 6.08734pt}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-27.33186pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt}}_{{\kern-10.66515pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 4.99846pt}}}r_{i}\left|\rho\right)
,

\forall\rho\in\widehat{X}
. Similarly, one concludes that

\left(b\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathchoice{=^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{X}}}}}{=^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{X}}}}}{=^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{X}}}}}{=^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{X}}}}}r_{i}\left(b\right|
,

\forall b\in\widehat{Y}
. These two last conditions imply that

\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.42075pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-11.75403pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 6.08734pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-27.33186pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.66515pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 4.99846pt{\widehat{X}}}}}r_{i}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
,
corresponding to atomicity of
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
upon

\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
. Also in this case the proof follows
straightforwardly from that of Theorem
1
. The opposite
implication is trivial.

\,\blacksquare

Analogously, one can generalize the other
necessary and sufficient conditions of Section
3.1
. One
can also provide only sufficient conditions for no-information without
disturbance. An example is given in the following proposition:

Proposition 6
.

An OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance upon

\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
, with
X\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A})}}
,

Y\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A})}}
, if there exists a pure state

\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\widehat{X}}
that is faithful upon

\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
. Similarly, an OPT satisfies
no-information without disturbance upon
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
if
there exists an atomic effect
b\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\widehat{Y}}
that is
faithful upon
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
.

Proof.

We explicitly prove the case of faithful state, since
that of faithful effect follows by analogy. Given a system
{\rm A}
,
let
\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\widehat{X}}
be pure and faithful upon

\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
(see Definition
23
). Now
let the test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
be
non-disturbing upon
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
, namely

\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.42075pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-11.75403pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 6.08734pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-27.33186pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.66515pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 4.99846pt{\widehat{X}}}}}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
.
Then, since
\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\widehat{X}}
we have

\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)=\left|\Psi\right)
, and since
\Psi
is pure, there exists a
set of probabilities
\{p_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
such that

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)=p_{i}\left|\Psi\right)
. However, due to the faithfulness of

\Psi
, the map
\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi\right)
is injective upon

\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
, and we conclude that

\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-30.74855pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.41515pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-14.08183pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 8.41515pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-28.42075pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.08734pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-11.75403pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 6.08734pt{\widehat{X}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\widehat{Y}}}}}=^{{\kern-27.33186pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.99846pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[5.55557pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.66515pt{\widehat{Y}}\kern 4.99846pt{\widehat{X}}}}}p_{i}\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
, and,
by definition, the test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
does
not extract information upon
\widehat{X},\widehat{Y}
.

\,\blacksquare

As a corollary one has a sufficient condition for no-information
without disturbance with no restrictions on
inputs and outputs:

Corollary 2
.

An OPT satisfies no-information without disturbance if for every
system
{\rm A}
there exists a pure state faithful for
{\rm A}
or an
atomic effect faithful for
{\rm A}
.

5
Outlook on no-information without disturbance

In this last section we analyse the relation between
no-information without disturbance and other properties of operational
probabilistic theories. We focus on local discriminability (see
Definition
9
) and purification (see
Definitions
11
and

12
) that, being typical quantum features,
are commonly associated with no-information without disturbance. Here
instead we show that no-information without disturbance can actually
be satisfied independently of the above two properties.

No-information without disturbance vs. purification

The following proposition proves that the probabilistic
theory
[
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
31
]

corresponding to the PR-boxes model, originally introduced in
Ref.
[
32
]
, satisfies no-information without
disturbance.

Proposition 7
.

The PR-boxes theory satisfies no-information without disturbance.

Proof.

This can be proved in several ways. For example we show that any
system of the theory allows for a reversible atomic transformation
and then use Proposition
3
. The fact that any
system has a reversible atomic transformation follows from the
following three points. I) The reversible transformations of the
elementary system
{\rm A}
of the theory (the convex set of normalized
states of
{\rm A}
is represented by a square, and the set of
reversible transformations of
{\rm A}
coincides with the set of
symmetries of a square, the dihedral group of order eight
D_{8}
,
containing four rotations and four reflections) are
atomic
[
29
]
. II) From
Refs.
[
33
,
34
]
we know that the
set of reversible maps of the
N
-partite system
{\rm A}^{\otimes N}

is generated by local reversible operations plus permutations of the
systems. Accordingly, the system
{\rm A}^{\otimes N}
allows for a
multipartite reversible transformation
U_{1}\otimes U_{2}\otimes U_{N}

made of local reversible transformations
U_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots N
. III)
Since PR-boxes satisfy local discriminability, the chosen
multipartite transformation is atomic due to the atomicity of
parallel composition (see Proposition
2
).

\,\blacksquare

As a corollary one has that also PR-boxes with minimal tensor product
satisfy no-information without disturbance.

Corollary 3
.

The PR-boxes theory with minimal tensor product satisfies
no-information without disturbance.

Proof.

Consider PR-boxes theory with minimal tensor
product
[
14
]
. We remind that in a probabilistic
framework, the minimal tensor product of state sets (or effect sets)
of two systems is the operation that yields the set of states (or
effects) of the composite system as containing only product states
(or product effects) and their probabilistic mixtures. The PR-boxes
theory with minimal tensor product has the same elementary system as
the PR-boxes theory (with the convex set of normalized states
represented by a square), but with composite systems constrained by
the minimal tensor product for both states and effects. The proof
that this probabilistic theory satisfies no-information without
disturbance is as in Proposition
7
(one proves that
every system has a reversible atomic transformation and
no-information without disturbance follows from
Proposition
3
).

\,\blacksquare

We can now establish the independence between no-information without
disturbance and purification (see also Figure
1
).

Proposition 8
.

No-information without disturbance and states or effects
purification are independent.

Proof.

We prove that:

1.

Purification
\nRightarrow
No-information
without disturbance.

2.

No-information without disturbance

\nRightarrow
Purification

1
. Consider deterministic classical theory. This is
classical theory where the probabilities of outcomes in any test are
either 0 or 1 (see also Ref.
[
10
]
). One can
easily see that in this probabilistic theory all states are pure and
all effects are atomic. As a consequence the theory satisfies both
states and effects purification according to
Definitions
11

and
12
. On the other hand, in this theory
all information can be extracted without disturbance.

2
. Consider PR-boxes theory with minimal tensor
product. On one hand this probabilistic theory satisfies
no-information without disturbance (see
Corollary
3
). On the other
hand, one can check that the theory satisfies neither states
purification, nor effects purification. First notice that, due to the
minimal tensor product prescription, multipartite pure states and
atomic effects are tensor products of local pure states and local
atomic effects, respectively. Therefore, given a multipartite pure
state (atomic effect) all its marginals are also pure states (atomic
effects). It follows that a state (effect) that is not pure (atomic)
cannot admit of any pure (atomic) dilation. Since the elementary system
of PR-boxes with minimal tensor product includes both non pure states
and non atomic effects, the theory does not satisfy states or effects
purification.

\,\blacksquare

Figure 1:
Comparing OPTs that satisfy no-information without
disturbance (grey set), local discriminability (red set) and states
or effects purification (blue set). Quantum theory (QT) lies at the
intersection of the three sets. As proved in
Proposition
8
no-information without
disturbance and purification are independent features. An example of
OPT that satisfies no-information without disturbance but violates
purification is the PR-boxes theory with minimal tensor product
(PR). Moreover, PR-boxes satisfy local discriminability, providing a
non-trivial intersection between local discriminability and
no-information without disturbance in the absence of
purification. On the other hand determinisctic classical theory
(DCT) is an example of OPT that satisfies purification but violates
no-information without disturbance. As proved in
Proposition
9
the set of convex OPTs
with purification is a proper subset of OPTs with no-information
without disturbance. We observe that no-information without
disturbance is also independent of local discriminability, as proved
in Proposition
10
. Indeed classical theory
(CT) satisfies only local discriminability while Fermionic quantum
theory (FQT) and real quantum theory (RQT) satisfy only
no-information without disturbance. Finally, it has been shown in
Ref.
[
35
]
that there exist OPTs without local
discriminability, that have all systems classical, thus retaining
the possibility of extracting all the information without
disturbance. An example is the
bilocal classical theory
(BCT)
of the same Ref.
[
35
]
, which satisfies 2-local
discriminability (see Definiton
10
).

While purification is not enough to imply no-information without
disturbance, in the next proposition we show that convex
OPTs
3
3

3

Actually one might relax the convexity hypothesis with
the following weaker condition:
If two states belong to a
jointly perfectly discriminable set, there exists an element of
the set that can be convexly mixed with both.
In particular, this
is the case if some convex combination of the two states is a state
of the theory.
that satisfy states or effect purification are a
subset of the OPTs with no-information without disturbance (actually
they are a proper subset, see also Fig.
1
). This provides
another useful sufficient condition for no-information without
disturbance (see also Figure
1
).

Proposition 9
.

A convex OPT with states purification or effects purification
satisfies no-information without disturbance.

Proof.

We consider the case of states purification (see
Definition
11
), with the proof for
effects purification (see Definition
12
)
following by analogy according to Remark
1
.

Given a convex OPT with states purification suppose that it violates
no-information without disturbance, namely there exists a system

{\rm A}
such that
\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}
is not atomic. Then let

\mathcal{I}_{\rm A}=\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, for some atomic non redundant test

\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}\subseteq{\mathsf{Transf}}({\rm A})
. Let us consider a mixed
state
{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})\ni\left|\rho\right)=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}p_{i}\left|\sigma_{i}\right)
with

p_{i}\left|\sigma_{i}\right)\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}=\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\rho\right)
, and
\{p_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
a
probability distribution with
p_{i}>0

\forall i
. Then by
Theorem
2
we have

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\sigma_{j}\right)=\delta_{ij}\left|\sigma_{i}\right)
. Since the theory allows
for purification, let
\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm B})}
be a purification of

\rho
for deterministic effect
e\in{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm B})
. Now, one one
hand since the test
\{\mathcal{A}_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
refines the identity, it
is
\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
, and
being
\Psi
pure it must be

\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=q_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
, with

\{q_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
a probability distribution. On the other hand,
for every
i\neq j
the marginals with deterministic effect

e\in{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm B})
of
\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
and

\mathcal{A}_{j}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
are perfectly discriminable. But this
contradicts the fact that
\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
and

\mathcal{A}_{j}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
are both proportional to
\Psi
.

\,\blacksquare

No-information without disturbance vs. local
discriminability

Turning to the case of local discriminability (see Definition

9
), we now show that it is independent
of no-information without disturbance (see also
Figure
1
).

Proposition 10
.

No-information without disturbance and local discriminability are
independent.

Proof.

We prove that:

1.

Local discriminability
\nRightarrow
No-information without
disturbance,

2.

No-information without disturbance
\nRightarrow

Local discriminability.

1
. Classical theory satisfies local discriminability but
violates no-information without disturbance, since in this theory all
information can be extracted without disturbance.

2
. Fermionic
[
16
,
18
,
17
]
and real quantum
theory
[
23
,
17
,
18
]
both
violate local discriminability as proved in
Refs.
[
23
,
17
,
18
]
where it is
also shown that they are both 2-local theories according to
Definition
10
. On the other hand Fermionic and real quantum
theories are convex theories with states purification and, due to
Proposition
9
, both satisfy no-information
without disturbance.

\,\blacksquare

Finally, we observe that as a consequence of
Corollary
3
, and subsequent
Remark
7
, the classical theory of
information is the only theory with local discriminability in which
all the information can be extracted without disturbance. However, in
the absence of local discriminability, it is still possible to have
other theories where all the information can be extracted without
disturbance. This has been proved in Ref.
[
35
]

where the authors describe an OPT whose systems of any dimension are
classical (and then violate no-information without disturbance), but
with a parallel composition that differs from the usual classical one,
leading to a violation of local discriminability, more precisely to a
2-local theory according to Definition
10
. This theory is
interesting because it provides an example of OPT that violates
simultaneously no-information without disturbance, local
discriminability and purification (see bilocal classical theory (BCT)
in Figure
1
).

6
Conclusions

We have analysed the interplay between information and disturbance for
a general operational probabilistic theory, considering the effect of
measurements also on correlations with the environment, differently
from the traditional approach focused only on the measured
system. Indeed, the two resulting notions of disturbance coincide only
in special cases, such as quantum theory, as well as every
theory that satisfies local discriminability. Our approach is
universal for any OPT, including theories without causality,
purification or convexity. In this setting we proved that the
atomicity of the identity transformation is an equivalent condition
for no-information without disturbance.

We have characterized the structure of theories where the identity is
not atomic, showing that in this case the information that can be
extracted without disturbance is “classical”, in the sense that it
is sharable and repeatable. On the other hand, we have established
that every OPT entails information whose extraction requires
disturbance, with the only exception of theories with all systems
classical.

While no-information without disturbance is a consequence of convexity
along with purification (purification of states or of effects), we
proved that purification and no-information without disturbance are
independent. Similarly, we have shown that no-information without
disturbance and local discriminability are independent properties.

Our results are expected to have immediate applicability to secure
key-distribution. Indeed, a physical theory including a system (or
even just a set of states of a system) that satisfies no-information
without disturbance can guarantee a private and reliable channel for
distributing messages. The idea of studying secure key-distribution in
a framework more general than the classical and the quantum ones has
been proposed in Refs.
[
12
,
14
]
. In
Ref.
[
12
]
it has been conjectured that in
every theory that is not classical secure key-distribution is
possible. The present generalisation of no-information without
disturbance to arbitrary OPTs is a first step in proving such a conjecture.

Acknowledgements.
This publication was made possible thanks to the
financial support of Elvia and Federico Faggin Foundation.

In the operational framework any event
\mathcal{A}
induces a map
between states. Consider for example the event

, the event

\Psi

A}}}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.88893pt\raise 3.00003pt\raise-3.41666pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 38.88895pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-6.04515pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{}^{{\rm B}}_{\phantom{{\rm B}}}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.88896pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-8.8889pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 5.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hphantom{\Psi}}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 18.88892pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-6.10417pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{{}^{{\rm C}}_{\phantom{{\rm C}}}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 28.88893pt\raise-11.49994pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{aligned}\ .

Accordingly, while states and effects are linear functionals over each
other, we can always look at an event as a map between states

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\mathop{\ordinarycolon}}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}\mapsto\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm C}}\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm B}{\rm C})},

(34)

(and similarly as a map between effects, from
{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
to

{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B}{\rm C})}
). The map above can be linearly extended to a map
from
{\mathsf{St}}_{\mathbb{R}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
to
{\mathsf{St}}_{\mathbb{R}}{({\rm B}{\rm C})}
(we denote
the extended map with the same symbol
\mathcal{A}
) and this extension is
unique. Indeed a linear combination of states of
{\rm A}{\rm C}
is null, say

\sum_{i}c_{i}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)
, if and only if
\sum_{i}c_{i}(a|\Psi_{i})=0

for any
a\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B}{\rm C})}
. Moreover, since

\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm A}{\rm C})}
for every
b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}
,
then
\forall b\in{\mathsf{Eff}}{({\rm B})}
we have that

0=\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\mathcal{A}(\sum_{i}c_{i}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}})=\sum_{i}c_{i}\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\left(b\right|_{\rm B}\sum_{i}c_{i}\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}
,
and we finally get
\sum_{i}c_{i}\mathcal{A}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=0
.

Appendix B
No-information test

We show that the weak condition
2
for no-information test is
equivalent to one in Definition
15
.

We focus now on no-information on the input (the case of
no-information on the output following by analogy) and show that
Eqs. (
9
) and (
11
) are
equivalent. We first prove that Eq. (
11
) implies
Eq. (
9
). To this end we evaluate the left
hand side of Eq. (
9
) using
Eq. (
11
), namely

\displaystyle\sum_{k}p(j,i,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})=\sum_{k}\left(c_{k}\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi_{j}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi_{j}\right)=p_{i}(e)(f|\Psi_{j}).

Now we notice that
p_{i}(e)
, which is a probability distribution on
the outcomes of the test
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
, also depends on the
deterministic effect
e=\sum_{k}c_{k}
. Therefore
p_{i}(e)
is a
probability distribution that depends on both the test

\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
and on the observation test
\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
while it
does not depend on the preparation test
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
, exactly as
the probability distribution
r
on the right hand side of
Eq. (
9
). Finally, we notice that

(f|\Psi_{j})
is a probability distribution on the outcomes of the
preparation test
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
, and the deterministic effect
f

can depend on both tests
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
and
\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
. We then
conclude that
(f|\Psi_{j})
is a probability distribution that
generally depends on all test
\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y}
,
\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X}
and

\bm{C}_{\rm Y}
, as the probability distribution
s
on the right hand
side of Eq. (
9
). Now we check the other
implication, namely that Eq. (
9
) implies
Eq. (
11
). Due to
Eq. (
9
), one has

\displaystyle\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi_{j}\right)=\sum_{k}\left(c_{k}\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}\left|\Psi_{j}\right)=\sum_{k}p(j,i,k|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})

(35)

\displaystyle=r(i|\bm{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})s(j|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z}).

First we notice that summing over the index
i
in the first and last member
of Eq. (
35
) we get

s(j|\bm{\Psi}_{\rm Y},\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})=(f|\Psi_{j})
,
where
\left(f\right|=\sum_{i}\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}
is a deterministic effect depending on tests

\bm{\mathcal{A}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
(indeed

\sum_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}
is a deterministic transformation). Therefore one has

\left(e\right|\mathcal{A}_{i}=r(i|\bm{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})\left(f\right|
. Since on
the left hand side of the last identity the only dependence on the observation test

\bm{C}_{\rm Z}
is through the deterministic effect
e
, one finally gets
that
r(i|\bm{\bm{\mathcal{A}}}_{\rm X},\bm{C}_{\rm Z})
is of the form
p_{i}(e)
,
as in Eq. (
11
).

Appendix C
Techical observation

Given a transformation
\mathcal{A}\in{\mathsf{Transf}}{({\rm A})}
such that for every
system
{\rm B}
there exists
p_{\rm B}
such that

\mathcal{A}\left|\rho\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=p_{\rm B}\left|\rho\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(36)

then actually
p_{\rm B}\equiv p
cannot depend on the system

{\rm B}
. Indeed, choosing
\rho=\tau\otimes\sigma_{\rm B}
with arbitrary

\tau\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
and normalised
\sigma\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm B})
, and
discarding system
{\rm B}
on both sides of Eq. (
36
)

\forall\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A}{\rm B})
, one obtains

\mathcal{A}\left|\tau\right)=p_{\rm B}\left|\tau\right)
. This clearly shows that
p_{\rm B}\equiv p
.

Appendix D
Techical lemma

Lemma 5
.

Given a state
\rho\in{\mathsf{St}}({\rm A})
one has

{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}}
, where
{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}

denotes the union of the refinements of any state in
D_{\rho}
. Given
an effect
a\in{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm A})
one has

{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{a}}\subseteq D_{{\mathsf{Ref}}_{a}}
, where
{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{a}}
denotes the
union of the refinements of any effect in
D_{a}
.

Proof.

Since the proof in the case of states and effects is
exactly the same (see Remark
1
) we focus on
the former. Consider a system
{\rm B}
and a state

\Psi\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm B})}
:

1.

\Psi\in D_{{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}}
iff

\left(e\right|_{{\rm B}}\left|\Psi\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\left|\sigma\right)_{\rm A}
, with

\sigma\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}
, and
e\in{\mathsf{Eff}}({\rm B})
deterministic.

2.

\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{D_{\rho}}
iff there exists a state

\Omega\in{\mathsf{St}}{({\rm A}{\rm B})}
, with
\Omega\in D_{\rho}
such that

\Psi\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\Omega}
, i.e. there exists a refinement

\{\Psi_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
of
\Omega
such that

\Psi\in\{\Psi_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
.

By hypothesis
\{\Psi_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
is a refinement of
\Omega
, namely

\left|\Omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\sum_{i\in{\rm X}}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}},

(37)

and
\Omega\in D_{\rho}
, namely
\left(e\right|_{{\rm B}}\left|\Omega\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\left|\rho\right)_{\rm A}
.
Accordingly, marginalising both sides of Eq. (
37
) one has that for every

i\in{\rm X}
,
\left(e\right|_{{\rm B}}\left|\Psi_{i}\right)_{{\rm A}{\rm B}}=\left|\sigma_{i}\right)_{\rm A}\in{\mathsf{Ref}}_{\rho}
. Since

\Psi\in\{\Psi_{i}\}_{i\in{\rm X}}
, this concludes the proof.

\,\blacksquare
